    for (int y = 0; y < 200; y++) {
        for (int x = 130; x < 135; x++) {
            f.z[y * 200 + x] = 1;
            fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + x)] = 255; fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + x) + 1] = 0; fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + x) + 2] = 0;
        }
        fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + 160)] = 0; fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + 160) + 1] = 0; fc.px[3 * (y * 200 + 160) + 2] = 255;
    }
    hm_render(&f, 30, &fc, &c, &o);
    ref_render(&f, 30, &fc, &c, &r);
    CHECK_INT(same_px(&o, &r), 40 * 40);
    int red = 0, blue = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 40 * 40; i++) { red += o.px[3 * i] == 255; blue += o.px[3 * i + 2] == 255 && o.px[3 * i] == 0; }
    CHECK(red > 40);
    CHECK_INT(blue, 0);
    img_free(&o); img_free(&r); img_free(&fc); hm_free(&f);

    /* real terrain from another angle, odd output size */
    Heightmap t = hm_new(128, 128);
    hm_fbm(&t, 3, 1.0f / 30, 6);
    hm_normalize(&t);
    float *n = malloc(sizeof(float) * 3 * 128 * 128);
    hm_normals(&t, 40, n);
    Image col = img_new(128, 128);
    hm_shade(&t, n, light_dir(200, 40), 0.3f, &col);
    VoxelCam c2 = {10, 20, 50, 0.6f, 5, 50, 200};
    Image a = img_new(77, 51), b = img_new(77, 51);
    hm_render(&t, 40, &col, &c2, &a);
    ref_render(&t, 40, &col, &c2, &b);
    CHECK(same_px(&a, &b) >= 77 * 51 * 97 / 100);
    int sky = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 77 * 51; i++) sky += a.px[3 * i] == 150 && a.px[3 * i + 1] == 190 && a.px[3 * i + 2] == 230;
    printf("sky %d\n", sky);
    CHECK(sky > 0 && sky < 77 * 51 / 2);
    img_free(&a); img_free(&b); img_free(&col); free(n); hm_free(&t);
    return TEST_END();
}

=== quiz
--- q
Your diamond-square fills random offsets with `rand()` in loop order. You change the loops to visit rows bottom-up. What happens?

--- choice
Nothing: the same seed gives the same terrain.

--- answer
The terrain changes: each point now receives a different number from the sequence. Hashing the point's coordinates with the seed makes the result independent of visiting order.

--- choice
It crashes, because diamond-square must run top-down.

--- choice
Only the corners change.

--- explain
A sequential PRNG hands out values in call order, so the *n*-th point visited gets the *n*-th number. Change the order and every point gets someone else's number. A coordinate hash gives each point its own value, which also makes the loop safe to parallelize.

=== quiz
--- q
Thermal erosion is implemented by adding each cell's outflow directly to its neighbors' heights during the scan. What artifact appears?

--- choice
None, the result is identical, only slower.

--- answer
A directional bias: material moved into a cell not yet visited in the same pass can be moved again, so slopes erode differently along the scan direction.

--- choice
Total mass is no longer conserved.

--- choice
The edges of the map rise.

--- explain
In-place updates make the pass order part of the algorithm. Mass is still conserved (every move subtracts and adds the same amount), but cells downstream of the scan see already-updated heights. Accumulating deltas and applying them after the pass makes every cell read the same snapshot.

=== quiz
--- q
How many indices does a heightmap mesh of 257 × 257 vertices need, with two triangles per grid quad?

--- choice
`257 * 257 * 6 = 396294`

--- answer
`256 * 256 * 6 = 393216`

--- choice
`256 * 256 * 2 = 131072`

--- choice
`257 * 257 * 3 = 198147`

--- explain
257 vertices per side make 256 quads per side, 65,536 quads, 2 triangles each, 3 indices per triangle. Knowing this before the loop is what lets you allocate once.

=== quiz
run: yes
--- q
What does this print?

--- code
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>

int main(void) {
    char buf[64] = "";
    size_t len = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++)
        len += snprintf(buf + len, sizeof buf - len, "v %d\n", i * 10);
    printf("%zu %zu\n", len, strlen(buf));
    printf("%d\n", snprintf(NULL, 0, "f %d//%d", 100, 100));
    return 0;
}

--- choice
13 13
11

--- answer
14 14
10

--- choice
14 14
0

--- choice
3 14
10

--- explain
`snprintf` returns the number of characters it wrote (excluding the `'\0'`): `"v 0\n"` is 4, `"v 10\n"` and `"v 20\n"` are 5 each, so `len` ends at 14, which is also where the terminator sits. With a `NULL` buffer and size 0 it writes nothing and returns the length the text *would* have: `"f 100//100"` is 10. That pair of behaviors is the whole "measure, allocate once, write" pattern.

=== quiz
--- q
In the droplet model, when does a droplet deposit sediment instead of eroding?

--- choice
Only when it stops moving.

--- choice
Whenever it moves downhill.

--- answer
When it carries more sediment than its capacity, or when it moves uphill (then it fills the pit, up to the height difference).

--- choice
When its water has fully evaporated.

--- explain
Capacity grows with downhill slope, speed and water volume. Slowing down on flatter ground lowers capacity below the carried sediment, so it drops some (alluvial fans and valley floors); going uphill means it is in a pit, which it fills.

=== quiz
--- q
Voxel Space draws each screen column front to back with a y-buffer. Why front to back?

--- answer
A nearer sample hides farther ones, so once a column's y-buffer is high enough, farther samples are rejected with a single compare and each pixel is painted once.

--- choice
Back to front would be wrong: the painter's algorithm cannot handle heightmaps.

--- choice
Because the heightmap is stored row-major.

--- choice
So that `dz` can shrink with distance.

--- explain
Back to front (painter's algorithm) also gives a correct image, but paints many pixels several times. Front to back with the per-column "highest row drawn" turns occlusion into a compare and enables early termination when every column reaches the top.
